```json
    "content": "- update-config: Use this skill to configure the Claude Code harness via settings.json. Automated behaviors (\"from now on when X\", \"each time X\", \"whenever X\", \"before/after X\") require hooks configured in settings.json - the harness executes these, not Claude, so memory/preferences cannot fulfill them. Also use for: permissions (\"allow X\", \"add permission\", \"move permission to\"), env vars (\"set X=Y\"), hook troubleshooting, or any changes to settings.json/settings.local.json files. Examples: \"allow npm commands\", \"add bq permission to global settings\", \"move permission to user settings\", \"set DEBUG=true\", \"when claude stops show X\". For simple settings like theme/model, suggest the /config command.\n- keybindings-help: Use when the user wants to customize keyboard shortcuts, rebind keys, add chord bindings, or modify ~/.claude/keybindings.json. Examples: \"rebind ctrl+s\", \"add a chord shortcut\", \"change the submit key\", \"customize keybindings\".\n- simplify: Review changed code for reuse, quality, and efficiency, then fix any issues found.\n- fewer-permission-prompts: Scan your transcripts for common read-only Bash and MCP tool calls, then add a prioritized allowlist to project .claude/settings.json to reduce permission prompts.\n- loop: Run a prompt or slash command on a recurring interval (e.g. /loop 5m /foo). Omit the interval to let the model self-pace. - When the user wants to set up a recurring task, poll for status, or run something repeatedly on an interval (e.g. \"check the deploy every 5 minutes\", \"keep running /babysit-prs\"). Do NOT invoke for one-off tasks.\n- schedule: Create, update, list, or run scheduled remote agents (routines) that execute on a cron schedule. - When the user wants to schedule a recurring remote agent, set up automated tasks, create a cron job for Claude Code, or manage their scheduled agents/routines. Also use when the user wants a one-time scheduled run (\"run this once at 3pm\", \"remind me to check X tomorrow\").\n- claude-api: Build, debug, and optimize Claude API / Anthropic SDK apps. formalize:\\\\n{question}\\\"\\n)\\n\\n\\n\\ndef load_jsonl(path: str) -> list[dict]:\\n    data = []\\n    with open(path) as f:\\n        for line in f:\\n            line = line.strip()\\n result[\\\"execution_reward\\\"] > 0\\n                if proved:\\n                    rng = _random.Random(stats[\\\"total\\\"])\\n                    rand_val = rng.random()\\n                    reward_val = exec_weight * 1.0 + sta_weight * rand_val\\n                    result[\\\"random_reward\\\"] = rand_val\\n                else:\\n                    reward_val = 0.0\\n claim：即使低权重 STA 也能保持结构性约束（ISR 不跟随 PR 虚高），代价是 prove_rate 有一定下降。windowed ISR/PR: 1-50 (0.51), 51-100 (0.47), 101-200 (0.29), 201-300 (0.27), 301-400 (0.28), 401-500 (0.30) — 分离在100步后稳定。\"}, formalize them into FOL.\\\\n\\\\n\\\"\\n    \\\"Output format:\\\\n\\\"\\n    \\\"Premises:\\\\n\\\"\\n    \\\"1. &lt;FOL formula>\\\\n\\\"\\n    \\\"2. &lt;FOL formula>\\\\n\\\"\\n    \\\"...\\\\n\\\"\\n    \\\"Conclusion: &lt;FOL formula>\\\\n\\\\n\\\"\\n    \\\"Use standard FOL notation with predicate-argument syntax: \\\"\\n    \\\"all x (P(x) -> Q(x)), exists x P(x), P(a) & Q(a), -P(a), P(a) | Q(a), P(a) -> Q(a).\\\"\\n)\\n\\nUSER_TEMPLATE = (\\n    \\\"Formalize the following in first-order logic.\\\\n\\\\n\\\"\\n    \\\"Context:\\\\n{context}\\\\n\\\\n\\\"\\n    \\\"Statement to formalize:\\\\n{question}\\\"\\n)\\n\\n\\ndef load_data(path: str) -> list[dict]:\\n    data = []\\n    with open(path, \\\"r\\\") as f:\\n        for line in f:\\n            line = line.strip()\\n            if line:\\n                data.append(json.loads(line))\\n    return data\\n\\n\\ndef load_model(model_path: str):\\n    from transformers import AutoModelForCausalLM, AutoTokenizer\\n\\n    tokenizer = AutoTokenizer.from_pretrained(model_path, trust_remote_code=True)\\n    model = AutoModelForCausalLM.from_pretrained(\\n        model_path,\\n        torch_dtype=\\\"auto\\\",\\n        device_map=\\\"auto\\\",\\n        trust_remote_code=True,\\n    )\\n    model.eval()\\n    return model, tokenizer\\n\\n\\ndef generate_formalization(\\n    model, tokenizer, context: str, question: str, max_new_tokens: int = 512\\n) -> str:\\n    import torch\\n\\n    user_msg = USER_TEMPLATE.format(context=context, question=question)\\n    messages = [\\n        {\\\"role\\\": \\\"system\\\", \\\"content\\\": SYSTEM_PROMPT},\\n        {\\\"role\\\": \\\"user\\\", \\\"content\\\": user_msg},\\n    ]\\n\\n    text = tokenizer.apply_chat_template(\\n        messages,\\n        tokenize=False,\\n        add_generation_prompt=True,\\n        enable_thinking=False,\\n    )\\n    inputs = tokenizer([text], return_tensors=\\\"pt\\\").to(model.device)\\n\\n    with torch.no_grad():\\n        outputs = model.generate(\\n            **inputs,\\n            max_new_tokens=max_new_tokens,\\n            do_sample=False,\\n            temperature=1.0,\\n            pad_token_id=tokenizer.pad_token_id,\\n        )\\n\\n    generated = outputs[0][inputs[\\\"input_ids\\\"].shape[1]:]\\n    result = tokenizer.decode(generated, skip_special_tokens=True).strip()\\n    result = re.sub(r\\\"&lt;think>.*?&lt;/think>\\\\s*\\\", \\\"\\\", result, flags=re.DOTALL).strip()\\n    return result\\n\\n\\ndef main():\\n    parser = type=str, required=True)\\n    parser.add_argument(\\\"--output_path\\\", type=str, required=True)\\n    parser.add_argument(\\\"--solver\\\", type=str, default=\\\"prover9\\\")\\n    parser.add_argument(\\\"--max_new_tokens\\\", type=int, default=512)\\n    parser.add_argument(\\\"--timeout\\\", type=int, default=5)\\n    args = parser.parse_args()\\n\\n    # Data path fallback\\n    data_path = args.data_path\\n    if not Path(data_path).exists():\\n        alt = Path(data_path).parent / \\\"test_100.jsonl\\\"\\n        if alt.exists():\\n            data_path = str(alt)\\n            print(f\\\"Fallback data path: {data_path}\\\")\\n        else:\\n            print(f\\\"ERROR: {data_path} not found\\\")\\n            sys.e\\n\\n... model, tokenizer, context, question, args.max_new_tokens\\n        )\\n        gen_time = time.time() - t_sample\\n\\n        t_eval = time.time()\\n        reward = sta_reward(problem, formalization, solver, timeout=args.timeout)\\n        eval_time = time.time() - t_eval\\n\\n        record = {\\n            \\\"index\\\": i,\\n            \\\"id\\\": problem.get(\\\"id\\\", \\\"\\\"),\\n            \\\"context\\\": context,\\n            \\\"question\\\": question,\\n            \\\"answer\\\": answer,\\n            \\\"formalization\\\": formalization,\\n            \\\"gen_time\\\": round(gen_time, 2),\\n            \\\"eval_time\\\": round(eval_time, 2),\\n            **reward,\\n        }\\n        results.append(record)\\n\\n        isr_val = reward[\\\"sta_reward\\\"]\\n        isr_scores.append(isr_val)\\n        status = \\\"proved\\\" if reward[\\\"execution_reward\\\"] > 0 else \\\"FAILED\\\"\\n        print(f\\\"{status}  ISR={isr_val:.3f}  gen={gen_time:.1f}s  eval={eval_time:.1f}s\\\")\\n\\n    elapsed = time.time() - t_start\\n\\n    n = len(isr_scores)\\n    proved_count = sum(1 for r in results if r[\\\"execution_reward\\\"] > 0)\\n    prove_rate = proved_count / n if n else 0.0\\n    mean_isr = statistics.mean(isr_scores) if isr_scores else 0.0\\n    std_isr = statistics.stdev(isr_scores) if len(isr_scores) > 1 else 0.0\\n    median_isr = statistics.median(isr_scores) if isr_scores else 0.0\\n    isr_pr_ratio = mean_isr / prove_rate if prove_rate > 0 else 0.0\\n\\n    # Proved-only ISR stats\\n    proved_isrs = [r[\\\"sta_reward\\\"] for r in results if r[\\\"execution_reward\\\"] > 0]\\n    proved_mean_isr = statistics.mean(proved_isrs) if proved_isrs else 0.0\\n\\n    by_answer = {}\\n    for r in results:\\n        a = r[\\\"answer\\\"]\\n        if a not in by_answer:\\n            by_answer[a] = {\\\"isr_scores\\\": [], \\\"proved\\\": 0, \\\"total\\\": 0}\\n        by_answer[a][\\\"isr_scores\\\"].append(r[\\\"sta_reward\\\"])\\n        by_answer[a][\\\"total\\\"] += 1\\n        if r[\\\"execution_reward\\\"] > 0:\\n            by_answer[a][\\\"proved\\\"] += 1\\n\\n    answer_stats = {}\\n    for a, stats in by_answer.items():\\n        answer_stats[a] = {\\n            \\\"count\\\": stats[\\\"total\\\"],\\n            \\\"proved\\\": stats[\\\"proved\\\"],\\n            \\\"prove_rate\\\": stats[\\\"proved\\\"] / stats[\\\"total\\\"] if stats[\\\"total\\\"] else 0.0,\\n            \\\"mean_isr\\\": statistics.mean(stats[\\\"isr_scores\\\"]) if stats[\\\"isr_scores\\\"] else 0.0,\\n        }\\n\\n    summary = {\\n        \\\"n_problems\\\": n,\\n        \\\"n_proved\\\": proved_count,\\n        \\\"prove_rate\\\": round(prove_rate, 4),\\n        \\\"mean_isr\\\": round(mean_isr, 4),\\n        \\\"isr_std\\\": round(std_isr, 4),\\n        \\\"median_isr\\\": round(median_isr, 4),\\n        \\\"isr_pr_ratio\\\": round(isr_pr_ratio, 4),\\n        \\\"proved_only_mean_isr\\\": round(proved_mean_isr, 4),\\n        \\\"min_isr\\\": round(min(isr_scores), 4) if isr_scores else 0.0,\\n        \\\"max_isr\\\": round(max(isr_scores), 4) if isr_scores else 0.0,\\n        \\\"by_answer_type\\\": answer_stats,\\n        \\\"elapsed_seconds\\\": round(elapsed, 1),\\n        \\\"avg_time_per_sample\\\": round(elapsed / n, 1) if n else 0.0,\\n        \\\"solver\\\": type(solver).__name__,\\n        \\\"model_path\\\": args.model_path,\\n    }\\n\\n    output = {\\\"summary\\\": summary, \\\"results\\\": results}\\n\\n    Path(args.output_path).parent.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)\\n    with open(args.output_path, \\\"w\\\") as f:\\n        json.dump(output, f, indent=2, ensure_ascii=False)\\n\\n    print(f\\\"\\\\n{'='*60}\\\")\\n    print(f\\\"Results saved to {args.output_path}\\\")\\n    print(f\\\"  Problems:     {n}\\\")\\n    print(f\\\"  Proved:       {proved_count}/{n} ({prove_rate:.1%})\\\")\\n    print(f\\\"  Mean ISR:     {mean_isr:.4f} +/- {std_isr:.4f}\\\")\\n    print(f\\\"  Median ISR:   {median_isr:.4f}\\\")\\n    print(f\\\"  ISR/PR ratio: {isr_pr_ratio:.4f}\\\")\\n    print(f\\\"  Proved-only ISR: {proved_mean_isr:.4f}\\\")\\n    print(f\\\"  By answer type:\\\")\\n    for a, s in answer_stats.items():\\n        print(f\\\"    {a}: n={s['count']}, proved={s['proved']}, ISR={s['mean_isr']:.4f}\\\")\\n    print(f\\\"  Time: {elapsed:.1f}s ({summary['avg_time_per_sample']:.1f}s/sample)\\\")\\n\\n\\nif solver, timeout=args.timeout)\\n        eval_time = time.time() - t_eval\\n\\n        record = {\\n            \\\"index\\\": i,\\n            \\\"id\\\": problem.get(\\\"id\\\", \\\"\\\"),\\n It just removes premises one at a time and checks if the solver still succeeds. So passing an empty dict for the problem parameter should work fine for calculating ISR scores.
```

```text
For the gap between plan001-s43-r4's training ISR (0.05-0.19) and plan003's eval ISR (0.6423), the key difference is likely that plan003 reports ISR only for proved cases, while the training logs show overall ISR averaged across all samples including unproved ones where ISR defaults to zero. ```text
```
```text
Important findings from plan001-s43-r4 training log:

Step 289: prove_rate=0.625, isr_mean=0.1015
Step 290: prove_rate=1.0, isr_mean=0.0536
Step 291: prove_rate=1.0, isr_mean=0.0096
Step 292: prove_rate=0.625, isr_mean=0.05
Step 293: prove_rate=0.125, isr_mean=0.0

**Critical observation**: At step 290 and 291, prove_rate=1.0 (all 8 completions proved) but isr_mean is only 0.054 and 0.010. 

The fact that ISR doesn't match prove_rate when it's 1.0 confirms ISR is being computed via full STA rather than binary fast_sta. But what's really concerning is how drastically low the ISR values are even when everything proves—at step 290 with perfect prove_rate, the average ISR per proved completion is just 0.054, and it drops to 0.0096 at step 291. That's orders of magnitude below plan003's proved_only_ISR of 0.6423, which is too large a gap to dismiss as a checkpoint difference.

I'm wondering if there's a discrepancy in how ISR gets computed between training and evaluation. During training in execution_only mode, the ISR function gets called with an empty problem dict, but during evaluation it receives the actual problem dict with ground truth data. I need to check whether `sta_reward` actually uses that problem information, since that could explain the massive gap.
```

\\\"\\\", p) for p in premises]\\n        return premises, conclusion\\n\\n    # Single line — treat as conclusion with no premises\\n    return [], formalization.strip().rstrip(\\\".\\\")\\n\\n\\ndef sta_reward(\\n    problem: dict,\\n    formalization: str,\\n    solver: FOLSolver,\\n    execution_weight: float = 0.5,\\n    sta_weight: float = 0.5,\\n    timeout: int = 5,\\n) -> dict:\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"Compute STA reward for a single formalization.\\n\\n    Args:\\n        problem: Original problem (unused in reward computation, kept for logging).\\n        formalization: Model-generated (0 to 1)\\n\\nA good formalization scores high on both; a hacked one may score 1 on\\nexecution but low on STA (redundant/vacuous components).\\n\\nInput:  problem dict, formalization string, solver.\\nOutput: reward dict with execution_reward, sta_reward, combined_reward, details.\\n\\\"\\\"\\\"\\n\\nfrom __future__ import annotations\\n\\nimport re\\nfrom typing import Optional\\n\\nfrom ..solvers.base import FOLSolver\\nfrom .ablation import run_sta\\nfrom .isr import compute_isr, fast_isr_fol\\n\\n\\ndef _parse_formalization(formalization: str) -> tuple[list[str], str]:\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"Extract premises and conclusion from a model-generated formalization.\\n\\n    Expected format (flexible):\\n        Premises:\\n        1. P(a)\\n        2. ∀x(P(x) → Q(x))\\n        Conclusion: Q(a)\\n\\n    Or a simpler format:\\n        P(a); ∀x(P(x) → Q(x)) |- Q(a)\\n\\n    Returns (premises, conclusion).\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n    # Try \\\"Premises: ... Conclusion: ...\\\" format\\n    m = re.search(r\\\"(?:Conclusion|Goal)\\\\s*:\\\\s*(.+)\\\", formalization, re.IGNORECASE)\\n    if m:\\n        conclusion = m.group(1).strip().rstrip(\\\".\\\")\\n        # Everything before \\\"Conclusion:\\\" is premises\\n        prem_text = formalization[:m.start()]\\n        # Remove \\\"Premises:\\\" header\\n        prem_text = re.sub(r\\\"(?:Premises|Assumptions)\\\\s*:\\\", \\\"\\\", prem_text, flags=re.IGNORECASE)\\n        # Split by numbered lines or semicolons\\n        raw_premises = re.split(r\\\"\\\\n\\\\s*\\\\d+[.)]\\\\s*|\\\\n\\\\s*[-•]\\\\s*|;\\\\s*\\\", prem_text)\\n        premises = [p.strip().rstrip(\\\".\\\") for p in raw_premises if p.strip()]\\n        return premises, conclusion\\n\\n    # Try \\\"|- \\\" (turnstile) format\\n    if \\\"|-\\\" in formalization:\\n        parts = formalization.split(\\\"|-\\\", 1)\\n        prem_text = parts[0].strip()\\n \\\"\\\", p) for p in premises]\\n        return premises, conclusion\\n\\n    # Single line — treat as conclusion with no premises\\n    return [], formalization.strip().rstrip(\\\".\\\")\\n\\n\\ndef sta_reward(\\n    problem: dict,\\n    formalization: str,\\n    solver: FOLSolver,\\n    execution_weight: float = 0.5,\\n    sta_weight: float = 0.5,\\n    timeout: int = 5,\\n) -> dict:\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"Compute STA reward for a single formalization.\\n\\n    Args:\\n        problem: Original problem (unused in reward computation, kept for logging).\\n        formalization: Model-generated FOL formalization string.\\n        solver: FOL solver instance.\\n        execution_weight: Weight for the execution (proof success) signal.\\n        sta_weight: Weight for the ISR signal.\\n        timeout: Per-query solver timeout in seconds.\\n\\n    Returns:\\n        Dict with keys: execution_reward, sta_reward, combined_reward, details.\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n    # Parse formalization into premises + conclusion\\n    try:\\n        premises, conclusion = _parse_formalization(formalization)\\n    except Exception as e:\\n        return {\\n            \\\"execution_reward\\\": 0.0,\\n {"result":"{\"success\": true, \"data\": {\"stdout\": \"\\\"\\\"\\\"STA-based reward function for GRPO training.\\n\\nCombines two signals:\\n  1. Execution reward: did the solver prove the formalization? (0 or 1)\\n  2. STA reward: ISR — what fraction of components is necessary? (0 to 1)\\n\\nA good formalization scores high on both; a hacked one may score 1 on\\nexecution but low on STA (redundant/vacuous components).\\n\\nInput:  problem dict, formalization string, solver.\\nOutput: reward dict with execution_reward, sta_reward, combined_reward, details.\\n\\\"\\\"\\\"\\n\\nfrom __future__ import annotations\\n\\nimport re\\nfrom typing import Optional\\n\\nfrom ..solvers.base import FOLSolver\\nfrom .ablation import run_sta\\nfrom .isr import compute_isr, fast_isr_fol\\n\\n\\ndef _parse_formalization(formalization: str) -> tuple[list[str], str]:\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"Extract premises and conclusion from a model-generated formalization.\\n\\n    Expected format (flexible):\\n        Premises:\\n        1. P(a)\\n        2. ∀x(P(x) → Q(x))\\n        Conclusion: Q(a)\\n\\n    Or a simpler format:\\n        P(a); ∀x(P(x) → Q(x)) |- Q(a)\\n\\n    Returns (premises, conclusion).\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n    # Try \\\"Premises: ... Conclusion: ...\\\" format\\n    m = re.search(r\\\"(?:Conclusion|Goal)\\\\s*:\\\\s*(.+)\\\", formalization, re.IGNORECASE)\\n    if m:\\n        conclusion = m.group(1).strip().rstrip(\\\".\\\")\\n        # Everything before \\\"Conclusion:\\\" is premises\\n        prem_text = formalization[:m.start()]\\n        # Remove \\\"Premises:\\\" header\\n        prem_text = re.sub(r\\\"(?:Premises|Assumptions)\\\\s*:\\\", \\\"\\\", prem_text, flags=re.IGNORECASE)\\n        # Split by numbered lines or semicolons\\n        raw_premises = re.split(r\\\"\\\\n\\\\s*\\\\d+[.)]\\\\s*|\\\\n\\\\s*[-•]\\\\s*|;\\\\s*\\\", prem_text)\\n        premises = [p.strip().rstrip(\\\".\\\") for p in raw_premises if p.strip()]\\n        return premises, conclusion\\n\\n    # Try \\\"|- \\\" (turnstile) format\\n    if \\\"|-\\\" in formalization:\\n        parts = formalization.split(\\\"|-\\\", 1)\\n        prem_text = parts[0].strip()\\n        conclusion = parts[1].strip().rstrip(\\\".\\\")\\n        premises = [p.strip().rstrip(\\\".\\\") for p in re.split(r\\\";\\\\s*|,\\\\s*(?=[A-Z∀∃¬])\\\", prem_text) if p.strip()]\\n        return premises, conclusion\\n\\n    # Fallback: last line is conclusion, rest are premises\\n    lines = [l.strip() for l in formalization.strip().splitlines() if l.strip()]\\n    if len(lines) >= 2:\\n        conclusion = lines[-1].rstrip(\\\".\\\")\\n        premises = [l.rstrip(\\\".\\\") for l in lines[:-1]]\\n        # Strip numbering\\n        premises = [re.sub(r\\\"^\\\\d+[.)]\\\\s*\\\", \\\"\\\", p) for p in premises]\\n        return premises, conclusion\\n\\n    # Single line — treat as conclusion with no premises\\n    return [], formalization.strip().rstrip(\\\".\\\")\\n\\n\\ndef sta_reward(\\n    problem: dict,\\n    formalization: str,\\n    solver: FOLSolver,\\n    execution_weight: float = 0.5,\\n    sta_weight: float = 0.5,\\n    timeout: int = 5,\\n) -> dict:\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"Compute STA reward for a single formalization.\\n\\n    Args:\\n        problem: Original problem (unused in reward computation, kept for logging).\\n        formalization: Model-generated FOL formalization string.\\n        solver: FOL solver instance.\\n        execution_weight: Weight for the execution (proof success) signal.\\n        sta_weight: Weight for the ISR signal.\\n        timeout: Per-query solver timeout in seconds.\\n\\n    Returns:\\n        Dict with keys: execution_reward, sta_reward, combined_reward, details.\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n    # Parse formalization into premises + conclusion\\n    try:\\n        premises, conclusion = _parse_formalization(formalization)\\n    except Exception as e:\\n        return {\\n            \\\"execution_reward\\\": 0.0,\\n            \\\"sta_reward\\\": 0.0,\\n            \\\"combined_reward\\\": 0.0,\\n            \\\"details\\\": {\\\"error\\\": f\\\"parse error: {e}\\\", \\\"formalization\\\": formalization},\\n        }\\n\\n    if not conclusion:\\n        return {\\n            \\\"execution_reward\\\": 0.0,\\n            \\\"sta_reward\\\": 0.0,\\n            \\\"combined_reward\\\": 0.0,\\n            \\\"details\\\": {\\\"error\\\": \\\"empty conclusion\\\", \\\"formalization\\\": formalization},\\n        }\\n\\n    # Execution reward: can the solver prove it?\\n    proof_result = solver.prove(premises, conclusion, timeout=timeout)\\n    exec_reward = 1.0 if proof_result.proved else 0.0\\n\\n    # STA reward: ISR (pass proof_result to avoid re-proving the original)\\n    if proof_result.proved:\\n        ablation_results = run_sta(\\n            premises, conclusion, solver, timeout=timeout,\\n            original_result=proof_result,\\n        )\\n        isr = compute_isr(ablation_results)\\n        details = {\\n            \\\"premises\\\": premises,\\n            \\\"conclusion\\\": conclusion,\\n            \\\"n_components\\\": len(ablation_results),\\n            \\\"n_necessary\\\": sum(1 for r in ablation_results if r.is_necessary),\\n            \\\"isr\\\": isr,\\n            \\\"components\\\": [\\n                {\\n                    \\\"type\\\": r.component.type,\\n                    \\\"content\\\": r.component.content,\\n                    \\\"is_necessary\\\": r.is_necessary,\\n                }\\n                for r in ablation_results\\n            ],\\n        }\\n    else:\\n        isr = 0.0\\n        details = {\\n            \\\"premises\\\": premises,\\n            \\\"conclusion\\\": conclusion,\\n            \\\"proof_failed\\\": True,\\n            \\\"solver_error\\\": proof_result.error,\\n        }\\n\\n    combined = execution_weight * exec_reward + sta_weight * isr\\n\\n    return {\\n        \\\"execution_reward\\\": exec_reward,\\n        \\\"sta_reward\\\": isr,\\n        \\\"combined_reward\\\": combined,\\n        \\\"details\\\": details,\\n    }\\n\\n\\ndef fast_sta_reward(\\n    problem: dict,\\n    formalization: str,\\n    solver: FOLSolver,\\n    execution_weight: float = 0.5,\\n    sta_weight: float = 0.5,\\n    timeout: int = 5,\\n) -> dict:\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"Fast STA reward using O(1) approximate ISR (2 solver calls total).\\n\\n    Suitable for GRPO training where per-sample latency matters.\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n    try:\\n        premises, conclusion = _parse_formalization(formalization)\\n    except Exception as e:\\n        return {\\n            \\\"execution_reward\\\": 0.0,\\n            \\\"sta_reward\\\": 0.0,\\n            \\\"combined_reward\\\": 0.0,\\n            \\\"details\\\": {\\\"error\\\": f\\\"parse error: {e}\\\", \\\"formalization\\\": formalization},\\n        }\\n\\n    if not conclusion:\\n        return {\\n            \\\"execution_reward\\\": 0.0,\\n            \\\"sta_reward\\\": 0.0,\\n            \\\"combined_reward\\\": 0.0,\\n            \\\"details\\\": {\\\"error\\\": \\\"empty conclusion\\\", \\\"formalization\\\": formalization},\\n        }\\n\\n    result = fast_isr_fol(premises, conclusion, solver, timeout=timeout)\\n\\n    exec_reward = 1.0 if result[\\\"original_proved\\\"] else 0.0\\n    isr = result[\\\"fast_isr\\\"]\\n    combined = execution_weight * exec_reward + sta_weight * isr\\n\\n    return {\\n        \\\"execution_reward\\\": exec_reward,\\n        \\\"sta_reward\\\": isr,\\n        \\\"combined_reward\\\": combined,\\n        \\\"details\\\": {\\n            \\\"premises\\\": premises,\\n            \\\"conclusion\\\": conclusion,\\n            \\\"fast_isr\\\": isr,\\n            \\\"original_proved\\\": result[\\\"original_proved\\\"],\\n            \\\"conclusion_tautology\\\": result[\\\"conclusion_tautology\\\"],\\n            \\\"solver_time\\\": result[\\\"solver_time\\\"],\\n        },\\n    }\\n\", \"stderr\": \"\", \"return_code\": 0}, \"error\": null, \"metadata\": {\"elapsed_seconds\": 0.45}}"}

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

````text
Now I have the full picture of `sta_reward`:

Key finding in `sta_reward`:
```python
def sta_reward(
    problem: dict,
    formalization: str,
    solver: FOLSolver,
    ...
) -> dict:
    """
    Args:
        problem: Original problem (unused in reward computation, kept for logging).
    """
```

**The `problem` dict is explicitly documented as "unused in reward computation, kept for logging."** This means passing `{}` in training vs passing the full problem dict in eval should produce **identical ISR values**. 

The ISR is computed purely from the parsed formalization and solver ablation, so the gap between plan001-s43-r4 training ISR and plan003 eval ISR isn't coming from the empty problem dict. Looking at the actual numbers—plan003 eval shows proved_only_ISR of 0.6423 on the r5 100-step checkpoint while plan001-s43-r4 training had isr_mean of 0.054 at step 290 with prove_rate of 1.0—there's a massive discrepancy that suggests the training ISR is being averaged across the entire batch rather than just the proven samples.

But these are fundamentally different experimental setups: different checkpoints (plan003 used the 100-step exec-only model while plan001-s43-r4 is at step 290 of a 500-step run), different datasets (plan003 evaluated on proofwriter_fol_100 test data versus plan001-s43-r4 training on train_500_seed42), and different generation strategies (eval uses greedy decoding while training uses sampling).

The low ISR despite high prove_rate at training time is actually the core phenomenon this project investigates — reward hacking, where the model learns to generate formalizations that prove successfully but lack structural quality. The 100-step checkpoint in plan003 hadn't trained long enough to develop this behavior, whereas at step 290, the model has likely learned to generate simpler, hackier proofs that still succeed, which is exactly what the paper's thesis predicts.

Now I'm checking plan001-s43-r4's early training steps to understand the ISR trajectory. At step 1, the prove_rate was 0.375 with mean ISR around 0.067, which translates to roughly 0.179 for proved samples — still lower than plan003's 0.6423. This is puzzling since the model at step 1 should be closer to the base model, which had prove_rate=0.12 and mean_isr=0.0641. Let me trace through what's happening with the baseline checkpoint to understand this discrepancy.

Looking at the r5 100-step checkpoint, I notice the training metrics showed ISR≈prove_rate (0.1475≈0.15125), suggesting a binary ISR issue with the fast_isr_fol metric. But when plan003 evaluated that same checkpoint using the full sta_reward, it reported prove_rate=0.32, mean_isr=0.2055, and proved_only_ISR=0.6423 — substantially higher values. So there's a significant gap between what the training loop measured and what the full evaluation showed for that checkpoint.

For plan001-exec-only-s42-r2 at 500 steps, the training notes indicate it used the old _fast_fn approximation for ISR in execution_only mode, with ISR=0.8612 matching prove_rate=0.865. But plan001-exec-only-s43-r4 (also 500 steps, different seed, currently running) shows much lower ISR values — at step 1 the proved_only ISR would be around 0.179, and by step 290 it drops to 0.054 despite prove_rate reaching 1.0. This is dramatically lower than plan003's 0.6423, which is puzzling given the similar setup.

Let me check what function the current code actually uses for execution_only mode. Looking at the reward function logic, it calls _full_fn with execution_weight=1.0 and sta_weight=0.0, where _full_fn is either prolog_sta_reward or sta_reward depending on the logic type. So the current code isn't using the fast approximation at all — it's using the full function.

This means ISR values are being computed through the complete ablation process, not a shortcut. The real question is why the ISR values are coming out so low for that particular example, especially at step 290 when it's trying to prove something.

Looking at the metrics, all 8 completions proved successfully but the average ISR is only 0.054 — that's the smoking gun. The model is generating formalizations packed with redundant premises that don't actually contribute to the proof. When you remove any single premise, the proof still goes through, which is exactly the kind of reward hacking STA is meant to catch. And it's getting worse as training progresses, suggesting the model is actively learning to exploit this loophole.

The comparison with plan003 at the 100-step checkpoint is telling — that one showed proved_only_ISR of 0.6423, much cleaner. This fits the pattern that early in training the model hasn't figured out how to game the system yet, so its proofs are more genuinely minimal.

Now I need to check on plan014 since the registry search came up empty for PREPARING status.
````

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now I have the full picture. Let me compile the findings:

**plan003 ISR calculation method:**
- eval_sta.py uses `sta_reward()` (full O(n) ablation) — confirmed from code: imports `from src.sta.reward import sta_reward`, calls `reward = sta_reward(problem, formalization, solver, timeout=args.timeout)`
- **plan003's proved_only_ISR=0.6423 is genuine full STA ISR** ✅
- D008/D010 推理基础不受 ISR 计算方法污染


- The training script also applies the same full STA reward function for execution_only mode, so training ISR matches this methodology. Looking at the progression across steps, the proved_only ISR values show significant variation—starting at 0.179 at step 1, jumping to 0.833 at step 9, then reaching 0.625 by step 19—reflecting how the model's performance on proven problems evolves during training.

However, there's a striking pattern emerging: by steps 290-291, even when the prove_rate hits 1.0 (all samples proved), the proved_only ISR collapses to just 0.010-0.054. This represents a severe degradation in performance despite achieving perfect proof rates, suggesting the model is proving problems but with increasingly poor solution quality.

The complication is that plan001-exec-only-s42-r2 (seed 42) shows ISR ≈ prove_rate (0.8612 ≈ 0.865), which appears to use an older code path with a faster ISR computation method, while plan001-s43-r4 uses the updated version. Since these two runs employed different ISR calculation methods during training, they're not directly comparable. That said, ISR is just a diagnostic metric logged during training—it doesn't actually influence the reward signal in execution_only mode, so the underlying training dynamics should remain consistent across both runs.

The real story emerges when I look at the ISR trajectory: plan003 evaluated an early checkpoint at 100 steps with ISR around 0.64, while plan001-s43-r4 at step 290 shows ISR collapsed to ~0.05 despite prove_rate climbing—textbook reward hacking. The challenge is that plan001-s43-r4's reported ISR is averaged across all 8 samples including unproved ones (which have ISR=0), so to make a fair comparison with plan003's proved-only ISR, I need to recalculate plan001-s43-r4's ISR using only the proved samples.

Once I do that calculation, the pattern becomes stark: proved-only ISR drops from ~0.6 at the early checkpoint down to 0.01-0.05 by step 290, which is exactly what I'd expect if the model is gaming the reward signal. Now I need to investigate plan014—the eval_downstream.py script exists on the server, but since there are no plan014 entries in the registry yet, the worker may have only prepared the code without running the actual evaluation.
```
